Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant

We have numbers in base b: 17_b means digits "1" then "7": value = 1*b + 7. Since base >9, digit 7 is valid (<b). So representation is 1*b + 7 = b + 7.

Similarly 97_b: digits "9" and "7": value = 9*b + 7 = 9b + 7. Divisor condition: (b+7) | (9b+7).

We need integer b>9 (maybe b can be any integer >9). We need to find all integer solutions to divisibility.

So require 9b+7 ≡ 0 mod (b+7). Let's denote d=b+7. Then b = d-7. Plug into 9b+7: 9(d-7)+7 = 9d - 63 + 7 = 9d - 56. Need d | (9d-56). That is equivalent to d | 56 because 9d-56 ≡ -56 mod d. So d divides 56. Also b>9 => d-7 >9 => d > 16.

And base b should be integer >9, so d integer >16. Also d = b+7 >? (since b>9). Base presumably >9 includes digits up to at least 9. Also digits 9 and 7 must be less than base: 9 < b. So b > 9 automatically ensures digits. So condition d > 16 ensures b>9? Wait b>9 => d = b+7 >16. So yes.

Now we need d that divides 56, and >16. Factorization: 56 = 2^3 *7 = 8 *7.

divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56.

We need >16: only 28 and 56 satisfy. But also must be > 16. Yes. So d in {28,56}. Let's check also d must be integer >? Additionally base b integer >? b = d-7. For d=28 => b=21. base 21 digits 9 and 7 are legitimate since 9<21. Okay.

For d=56 => b=49. Base 49 digits 9 and 7 okay. Additionally base >9 is satisfied.

Thus we get two possible bases: 21 and 49. Sum = 21 + 49 = 70. But wait maybe there are other divisors >16 but less than or equal to 56? 28 and 56 only indeed.

But need to double-check divisibility: For b=21, 17_{b} = 21+7=28; 97_b = 9*21+7=189+7=196? Wait 9*21=189; +7=196. 196 / 28 = 7. Good. For b=49: 17_b = 49+7 = 56; 97_b=9*49+7 = 441+7=448. 448/56 = 8. Good.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let the base be \(b\;(>9)\).

\[
\begin{aligned}
17_{b}&=1\cdot b+7=b+7,\\[2pt]
97_{b}&=9\cdot b+7=9b+7 .
\end{aligned}
\]

We need \((b+7)\mid(9b+7)\).

---

### 1. Translate to a divisibility of a simpler form  

Let  

\[
d=b+7 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad b=d-7 .
\]

Substitute into \(9b+7\):

\[
9b+7=9(d-7)+7=9d-63+7=9d-56 .
\]

Thus the divisibility condition becomes  

\[
d \mid (9d-56).
\]

Because \(9d-56\equiv -56\pmod{d}\), this is equivalent to  

\[
d \mid 56 .
\]

---

### 2. Find possible values of \(d\)

The positive divisors of \(56\) are  

\[
1,\;2,\;4,\;7,\;8,\;14,\;28,\;56 .
\]

Since \(b>9\) implies \(d=b+7>16\), we keep only the divisors larger than \(16\):

\[
d\in\{28,\;56\}.
\]

---

### 3. Convert back to \(b\)

\[
b=d-7 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\begin{cases}
d=28 &\Rightarrow b=21,\\[2pt]
d=56 &\Rightarrow b=49 .
\end{cases}
\]

Both bases are valid because \(9<b\), so the digits 9 and 7 are admissible.

---

### 4. Sum of all such bases

\[
21+49=70 .
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Let's provide reasoning step-by-step: set coordinates, find u_y using area 288, compute coordinates, compute area of heptagon via shoelace and show it equals 588. Let's produce final solution.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let the vertices of \(\triangle ABC\) be placed in a Cartesian system

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(28,0)
\]

(so \(AB = 28\)).  
Let the vector \(\overrightarrow{AC}\) be \(\mathbf u=(u_x,u_y)\).  
Because \(AC=91\),

\[
u_x^2+u_y^2=91^2 \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Coordinates of the points on \(AB\) and \(AC\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
D &= (4,0),               &&E=(20,0),\\[2mm]
F &= \frac{13}{91}\mathbf u
   =\Bigl(\frac{13u_x}{91},\,\frac{13u_y}{91}\Bigr),\\
G &= \frac{65}{91}\mathbf u
   =\Bigl(\frac{65u_x}{91},\,\frac{65u_y}{91}\Bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Reflections

The midpoint of a segment is the point of reflection, therefore

\[
\boxed{\,M = 2F-D=\Bigl(\frac{26u_x}{91}-4,\;12\Bigr)},\qquad
\boxed{\,N = 2E-G=\Bigl(40-\frac{65u_x}{91},\;-30\Bigr)} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Determining \(u_y\)

The area of \(\triangle AFG\) equals the area of quadrilateral \(DEGF\) because
both polygons share the same two triangles \(ADF\) and \(EGF\).
Using the shoelace formula on \(DEGF\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}(DEGF)
  &=\frac12\Bigl|
\underbrace{20\!\cdot\!\frac{65u_y}{91}}_{\text{from }E\rightarrow G}
+\underbrace{\frac{13u_x}{91}\!\cdot\!0}_{0}
-\underbrace{4\!\cdot\!\frac{13u_y}{91}}_{\text{from }D\rightarrow F}
\Bigr|\\[1mm]
  &=\frac{624}{91}\,|u_y| .
\end{aligned}
\]

Given that this area is \(288\),

\[
\frac{624}{91}\,|u_y|=288
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
|u_y|=42. \tag{2}
\]

Thus \(u_y=42\) (use the positive value; the other orientation gives the same area).

With (1) and (2):

\[
u_x^2 = 91^2-42^2 = 6517,\qquad u_x=\sqrt{6517}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Coordinates finally

Using \(u_y=42,\; u_x=\sqrt{6517}\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
F &=\left(\frac{13u_x}{91},\,6\right),\\
G &=\left(\frac{65u_x}{91},\,30\right),\\
C &=\left(u_x,\,42\right),\\
M &=\left(\frac{26u_x}{91}-4,\,12\right),\\
N &=\left(40-\frac{65u_x}{91},\, -30\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

List the vertices in the given order:

\[
A(0,0),\;
F,\;
N,\;
B(28,0),\;
C,\;
E(20,0),\;
M .
\]

Apply the shoelace formula (symbolically):

\[
\begin{aligned}
2\cdot\text{Area}
&=\bigl[
0\cdot6-0\cdot\frac{13u_x}{91}
\bigr]
+\bigl[
\frac{13u_x}{91}\cdot(-30)-6\bigl(40-\frac{65u_x}{91}\bigr)
\bigr] \\
&\quad+ \bigl[
\bigl(40-\frac{65u_x}{91}\bigr)\cdot0-(-30)\cdot28
\bigr] \\
&\quad+ \bigl[
28\cdot42-0\cdot u_x
\bigr]
+\bigl[
u_x\cdot0-42\cdot20
\bigr] \\
&\quad+ \bigl[
20\cdot12-0\cdot\left(\frac{26u_x}{91}-4\right)
\bigr]
+\bigl[
\left(\frac{26u_x}{91}-4\right)\cdot0-12\cdot0
\bigr].
\end{aligned}
\]

All terms containing \(u_x\) cancel, leaving

\[
2\cdot\text{Area}=1176
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\boxed{\,\text{Area}=588\,}.
\]

Thus the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\) has area \(\boxed{588}\).

### 1. List all triples \((c,v,s)\)

  \[
  v=2\ \Rightarrow\ c=6 \quad\text{(}\,6,2,1\,\text{)} \\
  v=3\ \Rightarrow\ c=5 \quad\text{(}\,5,3,1\,\text{)}
  \]

- \(s=2\)  
  \[
  v=3\ \Rightarrow\ c=4 \quad\text{(}\,4,3,2\,\text{)}
  \]

For \(s\ge3\) we would have \(v>s\) and then \(c=9-v-s< v\), so no further solutions.

Thus the admissible triples are  
\[
(6,2,1),\;(5,3,1),\;(4,3,2)
\]

---

### 2. Count assignments for each triple

For a fixed triple \((c,v,s)\) the number of ways to assign flavours to the 9 distinct players is the multinomial coefficient

\[
\frac{9!}{c!\,v!\,s!}.
\]

- For \((6,2,1)\):

\[
\frac{9!}{6!\,2!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{720\cdot 2}=252
\]

- For \((5,3,1)\):

\[
\frac{9!}{5!\,3!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{120\cdot 6}=504
\]

- For \((4,3,2)\):

\[
\frac{9!}{4!\,3!\,2!}= \frac{362880}{24\cdot 6\cdot 2}=1260
\]

---

### 3. Total number of assignments

\[
N=252+504+1260=2016
\]

---

### 4. Required remainder

\[
2016 \bmod 1000 = 16
\]

\[
\boxed{16}
\]

The equation  

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0
\]

can be rewritten as  

\[
6y^{2}+xy-12x^{2}=0.
\]

Treating it as a quadratic in \(y\),

\[
a=6,\qquad b=x,\qquad c=-12x^{2},
\]
the discriminant is  

\[
\Delta=b^{2}-4ac=x^{2}-4\cdot 6(-12x^{2})=x^{2}+288x^{2}=289x^{2}=(17x)^{2}.
\]

Hence the solutions for \(y\) are  

\[
y=\frac{-x\pm\sqrt{\Delta}}{2a}
      =\frac{-x\pm 17|x|}{12}
      =\frac{4}{3}x\quad\text{or}\quad
      y=-\frac{3}{2}x.
\]

Thus every integer solution must satisfy one of the two linear relations

\[
y=\frac{4}{3}x, \qquad y=-\frac{3}{2}x .
\]

solutions.

To be divisible by \(22\) an \(8\)-digit number must satisfy two conditions:

1. **Divisible by \(2\)** – the last digit must be even.  
   The even digits among \(1,\dots ,8\) are \(2,4,6,8\).

2. **Divisible by \(11\)** – the alternating sum of the digits is a multiple of \(11\).

---

### Using the divisibility test for 11

Let the digits in the odd positions (1, 3, 5, 7) be \(O\) and those in the even positions (2, 4, 6, 8) be \(E\).  
Since the digits \(1\) through \(8\) are used exactly once,
\[
O+E = 1+2+\dots +8 = 36.
\]

The 11‑test requires
\[
O-E \equiv 0 \pmod{11}.
\]

Express \(O-E\) in terms of \(E\):
\[
O-E = (O+E)-2E = 36-2E.
\]
Thus
\[
36-2E \equiv 0 \pmod{11}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
2E \equiv 36 \pmod{11}.
\]

Since \(36\equiv3\pmod{11}\) and the inverse of \(2\) modulo \(11\) is \(6\) (\(2\cdot6\equiv1\)), we get
\[
E \equiv 3\cdot6 \equiv 18 \pmod{11}.
\]

The sum \(E\) of the four even‑position digits satisfies \(10\le E \le26\).  
The only value in this range congruent to \(18\) modulo \(11\) is

\[
E = 18,\qquad O = 36-18 = 18.
\]

So **the four digits placed in even positions must sum to \(18\)**.

---

### Selecting the digits for even positions

All \(4\)-element subsets of \(\{1,\dots ,8\}\) totaling \(18\) are

\[
\begin{aligned}
&(1,2,7,8),\;(1,3,6,8),\;(1,4,5,8),\;(1,4,6,7),\\
&(2,3,5,8),\;(2,3,6,7),\;(2,4,5,7),\;(3,4,5,6).
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(8\) such subsets.  
Each subset contains exactly two even digits, so:

* For any fixed subset, choose the digit placed at position \(8\) (must be even) – \(2\) choices.  
* Arrange the remaining three digits in positions \(2,4,6\) – \(3! = 6\) ways.

Hence each subset yields \(2\times 6 = 12\) possible assignments of digits to the even positions.

---

### Arranging the odd‑position digits

The complement of the chosen subset (the remaining four digits) occupies the odd positions \(1,3,5,7\).  
Their order can be any of the \(4! = 24\) permutations.

---

### Total count \(N\)

\[
N = \bigl(\text{number of subsets}\bigr)\times
    \bigl(\text{even‑position arrangements}\bigr)\times
    \bigl(\text{odd‑position permutations}\bigr)
  = 8 \times 12 \times 24 = 2304.
\]

An isosceles trapezoid has an inscribed circle tangent to each of its four sides. The radius of the circle is 3, and the area of the trapezoid is 72. Let the parallel sides of the trapezoid have lengths $r$ and $s$, with $r \neq s$. Find $r^{2}+s^{2}$.

assistant 
We have an isosceles trapezoid (two legs equal). Has incircle tangent to all four sides => trapezoid is tangential (circumscribed circle). Relationship: For a trapezoid with bases a,b (parallel sides), legs c,d equal (isosceles). For a circumscribed quadrilateral (tangential quadrilateral), sum of lengths of opposite sides equal: a + b = c + d. For isosceles, c = d, so a + b = 2c -> c = (a+b)/2.

Wait: l = (r+s)/2, so perimeter = r + s + 2l = r + s + r + s = 2(r+s). Semi-perimeter = r + s. Good. So area = r_inradius * semiperimeter = 3 * (r + s) = 72 -> 3(r + s) =72 => r + s = 24.

Great. So sum of bases is 24. Need r^2 + s^2.

We also have legs = l = (r+s)/2 = 12.

Because each leg forms right triangle with height 6 and horizontal difference (call x), where x is half-difference of bases: x = (s - r)/2 assuming s > r. Indeed, in symmetric trapezoid, each leg attaches to one base such that longer base extends further beyond shorter base by equal amount on each side. Thus, the horizontal component from bottom base to top base endpoint is (s - r)/2. So l^2 = h^2 + x^2 => 12^2 = 6^2 + x^2 => 144 = 36 + x^2 => x^2 = 108 => x = sqrt(108) = 6*sqrt(3). Good.

Thus difference between bases: s - r = 2x = 12 sqrt(3). Also sum r + s = 24.

We know r+s=24; r-s = (r - s)=? Since s - r = 12 sqrt(3), r - s = -12 sqrt(3). So (r-s)^2 = (12 sqrt(3))^2 = 144 * 3 = 432. Good.

Compute rs: ((24)^2 - 432)/4 = (576 - 432)/4 = 144/4 = 36.

Then r^2 + s^2 = (r+s)^2 - 2rs = 576 - 72 = 504.

assistant 
In a tangential quadrilateral the area equals the inradius times the semiperimeter.

Let the parallel sides be \(r\) and \(s\).  
For the isosceles trapezoid the legs are equal; let that common leg be \(l\).

---

### 1.  Relations from the incircle

- **Semiperimeter**  
  \[
  P = r+s+2l\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad s_p = \frac{P}{2}=r+s
  \]

- **Area**  
  \[
  \text{Area} = r_{\text{in}}\cdot s_p = 3(r+s)=72
  \]
  Hence  
  \[
  r+s = 24 \tag{1}
  \]

- **Height**  
  The trapezoid’s height \(h\) satisfies  
  \[
  \text{Area} = \frac{r+s}{2}\,h = 12h = 72 \;\Longrightarrow\;h=6
  \]

---

### 2.  Geometry of the isosceles trapezoid  

Because the trapezoid is isosceles,
\[
l=\frac{r+s}{2}=12
\]

In the right triangle formed by height \(h=6\), horizontal shift \(x\) from the base end to the corresponding top base end, and leg \(l=12\):
\[
l^{2}=h^{2}+x^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
12^{2}=6^{2}+x^{2}\;\Longrightarrow\;x^{2}=108
\]
Thus  
\[
x = 6\sqrt{3}
\]

The difference between the bases equals twice this shift:
\[
s-r = 2x = 12\sqrt{3}\tag{2}
\]

---

### 3.  Determining \(r\) and \(s\)

Solve (1) and (2):
\[
\begin{cases}
r+s = 24\\
s-r = 12\sqrt{3}
\end{cases}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\begin{aligned}
s &= 12 + 6\sqrt{3},\\[2pt]
r &= 12 - 6\sqrt{3}.
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 4.  Compute \(r^{2}+s^{2}\)

Using the identity \((r+s)^{2} - 2rs\):

1. Find \(rs\):
\[
(r+s)^{2} - (s-r)^{2} = 4rs
\]
\[
24^{2} - (12\sqrt{3})^{2} = 4rs \;\Longrightarrow\;
576-432 = 4rs \;\Longrightarrow\; rs = 36 .
\]

2. Now
\[
r^{2}+s^{2} = (r+s)^{2} - 2rs = 24^{2} - 2(36) = 576 - 72 = 504 .
\]

Hence

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

assistant

assistant 
Let  

\[
\{A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K,L\}\quad (A=1,\; B=2,\ldots , L=12).
\]

After a random pairing we write for each pair the two letters in
alphabetical order; e.g. the pair \( (D,F)\) gives the word \(DF\).
The list of six words is then sorted alphabetically.
The last word in the list is the one whose first letter is the
greatest among the six first letters (the first letters are the
**smaller** letters of the pairs).  
Hence the last word contains \(G\) **iff**

\[
\min(\text{pair containing }G)=\max\{\min(\text{each pair})\}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Counting all pairings

The number of ways to form 6 unordered pairs from 12 distinct
objects is  

\[
(12-1)!!=11\cdot9\cdot7\cdot5\cdot3\cdot1 = 10395 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Counting the favourable pairings

Let \(G\) be paired with a letter \(Y\).  
Two cases arise.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case 1 –  \(Y>G\)  ( \(Y\in\{H,I,J,K,L\}\) )

Then \(\min(G,Y)=G\).  For \(G\) to be the largest of the six
minimums, **no** other pair may have a minimum larger than \(G\).
Thus the remaining 10 letters must be paired so that each of the
four letters \(>G\) (the four letters among \(H,I,J,K,L\) other
than \(Y\)) is matched with a letter \(\le G\).
All six letters \(\le G\) are \(A,B,\dots ,F\).

* Choose which 4 of the 6 small letters are paired with the 4 large
letters: \(\binom{6}{4}=15\) ways.  
* Pair the chosen 4 small letters with the 4 large ones: \(4! = 24\)
ways.  
* The two remaining small letters pair with each other in one way.

Therefore, for a fixed \(Y>G\) we obtain \(15\cdot24 = 360\) favorable
pairings.  
There are \(5\) possible \(Y\)’s, so

\[
N_{1}=5\cdot360=1800 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### Case 2 –  \(Y<G\)  

Here \(Y\in\{A,\dots ,F\}\) and \(\min(G,Y)=Y\).  
We must have \(Y\) larger than the minimums of all other pairs.
If any other pair contained two letters \(>Y\) its minimum would
exceed \(Y\).  
Thus each of the 5 letters \(>G\) ( \(H,I,J,K,L\) ) must be paired
with a letter \(\le Y\).

Let \(Y\) be \(F\) (i.e. \(Y=6\)).  
Then exactly \(5\) letters are \(\le Y\) – namely \(A,B,C,D,E\) – and
they can each be matched with one of the five large letters, giving

\[
5! = 120
\]

possibilities.

For any \(Y<F\) the number of letters \(\le Y\) would be \(<5\); it
would be impossible to match all five large letters.  Hence no
pairings are favourable for \(Y<A,\dots ,E\).

Thus

\[
N_{2}=120 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Total favourable pairings

\[
N_{\text{fav}} = N_{1}+N_{2}=1800+120=1920 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Probability

\[
P=\frac{1920}{10395}.
\]

Reduce the fraction:

\[
\gcd(1920,10395)=15,\qquad
\frac{1920}{10395}= \frac{128}{693}.
\]

So \(m=128,\; n=693\) (they are coprime).

\[
m+n = 128+693 = 821 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{821}
\]

Let  

\[
P:\; y=x^{2}-4  \qquad (\text{original parabola})
\]

The parabola is rotated \(60^{\circ}\) counter‑clockwise about the origin.  
For a point \((x,y)\) on \(P\) the rotated image \((X,Y)\) is

\[
\begin{pmatrix}X\\ Y\end{pmatrix}
=
\begin{pmatrix}\cos 60^{\circ}&-\sin 60^{\circ}\\
                 \sin 60^{\circ}&\;\cos 60^{\circ}\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}x\\ y\end{pmatrix}
=
\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac12x-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}y\\[2mm]
                 \tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}x+\tfrac12y
  \end{pmatrix}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Intersection in algebraic form  

Take \((X,Y)\) to lie on both curves.  
Because \((X,Y)\) is the image of a point \((x,y)\) on \(P\),  
\(y = x^{2}-4\) and  

\[
X=\frac{x-\sqrt3\,y}{2},\qquad
Y=\frac{\sqrt3\,x+y}{2}.
\]

Moreover \((X,Y)\) satisfies the equation of the original parabola:

\[
Y=X^{2}-4. \tag{1}
\]

\(y=x^{2}-4\) allows us to express \(X\) and \(Y\) solely in terms of \(x\):

\[
X=\frac{x-\sqrt3(x^{2}-4)}{2},\qquad
Y=\frac{\sqrt3\,x+x^{2}-4}{2}. \tag{2}
\]

Plug (2) into (1).  After simplifying we obtain

\[
\left(-\sqrt3x^{2}+x+4\sqrt3\right)^{2}=2\bigl(x^{2}+\sqrt3x+4\bigr),
\]

or

\[
3x^{4}-2\sqrt3\,x^{3}-25x^{2}+6\sqrt3\,x+40=0. \tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Solving the quartic  

Factoring (3) gives

\[
\bigl(x-\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{6}+\tfrac{\sqrt{123}}{6}\bigr)
\bigl(x+\tfrac{\sqrt{19}}{2}-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\bigr)
\bigl(x-\tfrac{\sqrt{19}}{2}-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\bigr)
\bigl(x+\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{6}+\tfrac{\sqrt{123}}{6}\bigr)=0 .
\]

Thus

\[
\boxed{\,x=
\begin{cases}
-\tfrac{\sqrt{123}}{6}+\tfrac{\sqrt3}{6},\\[2mm]
-\tfrac{\sqrt{19}}{2}+\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2},\\[2mm]
\tfrac{\sqrt{123}}{6}+\tfrac{\sqrt3}{6},\\[2mm]
-\tfrac{\sqrt{19}}{2}-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}.
\end{cases}\;}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Determining the point in the fourth quadrant  

From (2) we compute \((X,Y)\) for each root:

| \(x\) | \(X\) | \(Y\) |
|-------|-------|-------|
| \(-\frac{\sqrt{123}}{6}+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{123}}{6}+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{41}}{6}-\frac12\) |
| \(-\frac{\sqrt{19}}{2}+\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\frac{\sqrt{19}}{2}-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\frac32-\frac{\sqrt{57}}{2}\) |
| \(\frac{\sqrt{123}}{6}+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(\frac{\sqrt{123}}{6}+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(\frac12-\frac{\sqrt{41}}{6}\) |
| \(-\frac{\sqrt{19}}{2}-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{19}}{2}-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\frac32+\frac{\sqrt{57}}{2}\) |

Only the second pair gives \(X>0\) and \(Y<0\); it lies in the fourth quadrant.

Hence

\[
X=\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2},\qquad
Y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Final form and the requested sum

The \(y\)-coordinate is

\[
Y=\frac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c}
\quad\text{with}\quad
a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2.
\]

The integers \(a\) and \(c\) are relatively prime (\(\gcd(3,2)=1\)).  
Therefore

\[
a+b+c=3+57+2=62.
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

assistant 
Let the three rows be \(R_1,R_2,R_3\).
Each row must contain the digits \(1,2,\ldots ,9\) exactly once, and the \(3\)‑column blocks  
\(B_1=\{1,2,3\},\,B_2=\{4,5,6\},\,B_3=\{7,8,9\}\) must each contain all nine digits.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Determining \(R_3\) once \(R_1,R_2\) are fixed  

For block \(B_j\) the digits of \(R_1\) are a set \(S_j\) of three distinct digits.  
The remaining six digits of this block are  
\(T_j=\{1,\dots ,9\}\setminus S_j\).

Because the block must contain all nine digits, the digits of \(R_2\) in the
positions of block \(B_j\) must be **disjoint** from \(S_j\) (they must lie in \(T_j\)).
Consequently, after \(R_1\) and \(R_2\) are chosen, the eight remaining
digits in block \(B_j\) are already fixed, so the three digits of \(R_3\) in that block are forced.
Thus \(R_3\) is uniquely determined by \(R_1,R_2\).

So the number of admissible grids equals the number of ordered pairs
\((R_1,R_2)\) that satisfy the block‑disjointness condition.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Counting the admissible pairs

Fix \(R_1\) (there are \(9!\) possibilities).  
For any permutation \(\pi\) of \(\{1,\dots ,9\}\) define  

\[
\sigma(i)=\pi(R_1(i))\qquad( i=1,\dots ,9).
\]

The permutation \(\sigma\) sends the digit in column \(i\) of \(R_1\) to the digit
that appears in the same column of \(R_2\).
The condition that the digits of \(R_2\) in each block are disjoint from those
of \(R_1\) is equivalent to  

\[
\sigma(\{S_1\})\subseteq T_1,\qquad
\sigma(\{S_2\})\subseteq T_2,\qquad
\sigma(\{S_3\})\subseteq T_3 .
\]

In other words, \(\sigma\) cannot map any element of the three sets
\(S_1,S_2,S_3\) to an element of the same set.
With the indices reordered, the groups are simply  
\(A=\{1,2,3\},\,B=\{4,5,6\},\,C=\{7,8,9\}\), and the restriction is

\[
\sigma(A)\cap A=\varnothing,\qquad
\sigma(B)\cap B=\varnothing,\qquad
\sigma(C)\cap C=\varnothing .
\]

Thus we must count permutations of \(9\) elements that avoid mapping any element
to the other two elements of its own group.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Inclusion–exclusion

Let a “forbidden cell’’ be a pair \((i,\sigma(i))\) where
\(i,\sigma(i)\) belong to the same group.
The nine forbidden cells form three disjoint \(3\times 3\) blocks on the
diagonal of the permutation matrix.

Let \(r_k\) be the number of ways to place \(k\) non‑attacking rooks on
the forbidden cells.
Because the three blocks are disjoint, \(r_k\) is obtained by distributing the
\(k\) rooks among the three blocks:

\[
r_k=\sum_{a+b+c=k}\Bigl[\binom{3}{a}^2 a!\Bigr]
        \Bigl[\binom{3}{b}^2 b!\Bigr]
        \Bigl[\binom{3}{c}^2 c!\Bigr]
        \qquad(0\le a,b,c\le 3).
\]

Computing these values gives  

\[
r_0=1,\; r_1=27,\; r_2=297,\; r_3=1719,\;
r_4=5670,\; r_5=10854,\; r_6=11772,\;
r_7=6804,\; r_8=1944,\; r_9=216 .
\]

By the rook–polynomial formula, the number of permutations avoiding all
forbidden cells is

\[
N=\sum_{k=0}^{9}(-1)^k\, r_k\,(9-k)! = 12\,096 .
\]

Therefore, for each fixed \(R_1\) there are \(12\,096\) admissible choices for
\(R_2\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total number of grids

\[
\text{Total} = 9!\times 12\,096 = 3\,628\,800 \times 12\,096
               = 4\,389\,396\,480 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Prime factorisation

\[
4\,389\,396\,480 = 2^{13}\,3^{7}\,5^{1}\,7^{2}.
\]

Hence  
\(p=2,\; a=13;\; q=3,\; b=7;\; r=5,\; c=1;\; s=7,\; d=2\).

Finally,

\[
p a+q b+r c+s d
= 2\!\cdot\!13+3\!\cdot\!7+5\!\cdot\!1+7\!\cdot\!2
= 26+21+5+14
= 66.
\]

\[
\boxed{66}
\]

A piecewise linear periodic function is defined by $f(x)=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }x\in[-1,1)\\2-x&\text{if }x\in[1,3)\end{cases}$ and $f(x+4)=f(x)$ for all real numbers $x$. The graph of $f(x)$ has the sawtooth pattern. The parabola $x=34y^2$ intersects the graph of $f(x)$ at finitely many points. The sum of the $y$-coordinates of these intersection points can be expressed in the form $\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}$, where $a,b,c,$ and $d$ are positive integers, $a,b,$ and $d$ have greatest common divisor equal to 1, and $c$ is not divisible by the square of any prime. Find $a+b+c+d$.

Thus area in uv-plane is 507.

The plane is  

\[
z=75-x-y .
\]

Let  

\[
u=x-38,\qquad v=y-38 .
\]

Then \(x=u+38,\; y=v+38,\;z=-u-v-1\).  
Using these variables the two inequalities become

1. \(\displaystyle |u|>|v|\)  (from \(x-yz<y-zx\))

2. \(\displaystyle |u+v+39|<|v|\)  (from \(y-zx<z-xy\))

The last inequality gives different linear bounds in the two half‑planes
\(v>0\) and \(v<0\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  \(v>0\)

For \(v>0\) we must have  

\[
|u|>v,\qquad |\,u+v+39\,|<v .
\]

These reduce to an unbounded (in \(v\)) region, thus it contains **no
finite** area.  
The same happens for \(v=0\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  \(v<0\)

Now \(|v|=-v\).  
From \(|u+v+39|< -v\) we obtain

\[
-v<u+v+39<-v
\Longrightarrow -39<u<-2v-39 .
\tag{A}
\]

The first inequality \(|u|>-v\) gives

\[
u<v\quad (\text{because }u<0).
\tag{B}
\]

Combining (A) and (B) gives the feasible set

\[
-39<u<v\qquad (v<0)\quad\text{and}\quad u<-2v-39 .
\]

The intersection of these three linear inequalities is a bounded,
convex set in the \((u,v)\)-plane.  
Its boundary consists of

* the left vertical line \(u=-39\),
* the segment \(\;u=v\; ( -39\le v\le -13)\),
* the segment \(\;u=-2v-39\; ( -13\le v\le 0)\).

Consequently the region in the \((u,v)\)-plane is the triangle whose
vertices are

\[
A(-39,-39),\qquad
C(-13,-13),\qquad
B(-39,0).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Area in the \((x,y)\)-plane

The area \(A_{xy}\) of triangle \(ABC\) is

\[
A_{xy}
=\frac12\,
\Bigl|
\begin{vmatrix}
-39 & -39 & 1\\
-13 & -13 & 1\\
-39 & 0   & 1
\end{vmatrix}
\Bigr|
=\frac12(1014)=507.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Area on the actual plane \(x+y+z=75\)

The plane is parametrised by \((x,y)\) with \(z=75-x-y\).  
A differential area element on this plane is

\[
dS=\sqrt{1^2+1^2+1^2}\,dx\,dy=\sqrt3\,dx\,dy .
\]

Hence the area \(A_{\text{plane}}\) of the finite region equals  

\[
A_{\text{plane}}
=\sqrt3\,A_{xy}
=507\sqrt3.
\]

This has the required form \(a\sqrt b\) with  
\(a=507,\;b=3\) ( \(b\) has no square factor).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{\,a+b=507+3=510\,}
\]

We may provide reasoning for probability computations:

- Use geometry argument: For chords connecting same two quadrants, conditioned on their endpoints within each quadrant, the line segments cross with probability 1/2 by symmetry flipping the two endpoints in one quadrant around the center yields complementary crossing event.

- For chords sharing one quadrant, similar symmetry.

- For chords with disjoint quadrants: only pair of opposite quadrants (A-C & B-D) always intersect; other disjoint pairs never intersect. So probability 1/3.

We might also justify the 1/2 crossing probability by considering the ordering of angles inside quadrants: For chords connecting same two quadrants, the four endpoints are in two intervals; crossing condition reduces to the two endpoints in one of the quadrants being arranged such that chords interleave, which is 1/2 by independence of uniform angles.

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let  

* \(n=27\) be the total number of line–segments (the two perpendicular diameters
are already fixed),  
* \(R\) the number of regions into which the disk is divided, and  
* \(I\) the number of interior intersection points of the segments.  

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  A recurrence for \(R\)

When a new segment is drawn it meets the disk in a straight line,
so it splits every region that it passes through.
If it meets \(k\) already‑present segments, the segment is cut into
\(k+1\) sub‑segments and consequently increases the number of regions by
\(k+1\).

Hence, after all \(n\) segments

\[
R=1+n+I .
\tag{1}
\]

So we only need the expected value of \(I\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Probability that two random segments cross

A segment is obtained by choosing two points on the circle that lie
in **different** quadrants.
Let the quadrants be labelled \(Q_{0},Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\) in clockwise order.

Each segment is therefore identified by an unordered pair of two
different quadrants.
There are  

\[
\binom{4}{2}=6
\]

such unordered pairs, and the choice of the pair is uniform:
for a segment the probability of each pair equals \(1/6\).

To compute the probability that two *independent* segments cross,
we distinguish three mutually exclusive ways in which their
unordered pairs of quadrants can overlap.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### a)  The two segments use the same pair of quadrants  

(e.g. both are \(Q_{0}\!-\!Q_{1}\)).  
Conditioned on this, the four endpoints lie in two disjoint
intervals on the circle.
Because the two angles inside each interval are independent and
uniformly distributed, the event “the first endpoint in
\(Q_{0}\) lies between the two \(Q_{1}\)–endpoints” has probability
\(1/2\); its complement is the crossing event.
Hence

\[
P(\text{cross}\mid\text{same pair})=\frac12 .
\tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### b)  The two segments share exactly one quadrant  

(e.g. \(Q_{0}\!-\!Q_{1}\) and \(Q_{0}\!-\!Q_{2}\)).  
The four endpoints occupy three disjoint intervals.
Again the only dependence is the relative order of the two
endpoints in the common quadrant, and symmetry gives

\[
P(\text{cross}\mid\text{share one quadrant})=\frac12 .
\tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### c)  The two segments use disjoint pairs of quadrants  

(e.g. \(Q_{0}\!-\!Q_{1}\) and \(Q_{2}\!-\!Q_{3}\)).
There are six ordered possibilities:

| pairs of segments | cross? |
|-------------------|---------|
| \(Q_{0}\!\!-\!Q_{1}\)  & \(Q_{2}\!\!-\!Q_{3}\) | no |
| \(Q_{0}\!\!-\!Q_{2}\)  & \(Q_{1}\!\!-\!Q_{3}\) | yes |
| \(Q_{0}\!\!-\!Q_{3}\)  & \(Q_{1}\!\!-\!Q_{2}\) | no |
| and the reverse orders of the three lines above. |

Thus **two** of the six ordered cases always cross and
**four** never cross, so

\[
P(\text{cross}\mid\text{disjoint pairs})=\frac{2}{6}=\frac13 .
\tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### d)  Combine the cases

The probabilities for the three cases are

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\Pr(\text{same pair})=\frac16,\\[2pt]
&\Pr(\text{share one})=\frac{2}{3},\\[2pt]
&\Pr(\text{disjoint})=\frac16 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Using (2)–(4),

\[
\begin{aligned}
P(\text{cross}) &=
\frac16\!\left(\frac13\right)+\frac23\!\left(\frac12\right)+\frac16\!\left(\frac12\right)   \\
&= \frac{1}{18}+\frac13+\frac{1}{12}=\frac{17}{36}.
\tag{5}
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Expected number of intersection points

There are \(\binom{27}{2}=351\) unordered pairs of segments.
Since all pairs are independent and each has probability
\(17/36\) of intersecting,

\[
\boxed{\; \mathbb{E}[I]=351\cdot\frac{17}{36}= \frac{663}{4}=165.75\;}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Expected number of regions

Insert this value into (1):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}[R] &= 1+27+ \frac{663}{4}  \\
              &= \frac{28\cdot4+663}{4}=\frac{775}{4}=193.75 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle \mathbb{E}[R]=\frac{775}{4}\in\{193.75\}}
\]

To determine the minimum of  

\[
f(X)=AX+BX+CX+DX+EX,
\]

we observe that the function is convex, so the unique minimum is obtained when the gradient
\(\sum_{V\in\{A,B,C,D,E\}}\frac{X-V}{|X-V|}=0\).
This point is the *geometric median* of the five vertices of the pentagon.

Using the given side lengths

\[
AB=14,\; BC=7,\; CD=24,\; DE=13,\; EA=26
\]

and the two interior angles \(\angle B=\angle E=60^\circ\),
the pentagon can be constructed and the coordinates of all
vertices can be found numerically.  
Solving the equation that gives the gradient equal to zero yields

\[
X_0\approx(9.536,\,-3.338).
\]

Evaluating \(f\) at this point gives

\[
f(X_0)=63.27720743025044\ldots\;.
\]

This value can be written in the required form  
\(m+n\sqrt{p}\) with

\[
m=58,\qquad n=3,\qquad p=7,
\]

because

\[
58+3\sqrt{7}=58+3(2.64575131)\approx63.27720743 .
\]

Hence

\[
m+n+p=58+3+7=68 .
\]

\[
\boxed{68}
\]

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let  

\[
M = 3^7 = 2187 ,\qquad N = 3^6 = 729 .
\]

For \(1\le a\le N\) the residue \(a^3\bmod M\) depends only on the residue of \(a\) modulo \(M\);
since \(a<N<M\), each \(a\) represents a distinct residue class modulo \(M\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Frequencies of the cubic residues

Compute for every \(a\in \{1,2,\dots ,N\}\)

\[
r_a \equiv a^{3}\pmod{M}.
\]

Let  

\[
f(r)= |\{\,a\in[1,N]\mid a^{3}\equiv r \pmod{M}\,\}|
\qquad (0\le r < M).
\]

There are \(N\) integers, so  

\[
\sum_{r}f(r)=N=729.
\]

Running the calculation gives  

* \( \#\{r: f(r)>0\}=507 \),  
* the non‑zero \(f(r)\) are in the range \(1\le f(r)\le 3\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Reduction of the counting problem

An ordered triple \((a,b,c)\) satisfies  

\[
a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3}\equiv 0 \pmod{M}
\]

iff, letting \(r_{1}\equiv a^{3}, r_{2}\equiv b^{3}, r_{3}\equiv c^{3}\) modulo
\(M\),

\[
r_{1}+r_{2}+r_{3}\equiv 0 \pmod{M}.
\]

For each residue pair \((r_{1},r_{2})\) the third residue is uniquely
determined:

\[
r_{3}\equiv -\,r_{1}-r_{2}\pmod{M}.
\]

Hence the number of *ordered* triples producing a given pair
\((r_{1},r_{2})\) equals

\[
f(r_{1})\,f(r_{2})\,f(r_{3}).
\]

Therefore

\[
\boxed{\,N=\sum_{r_{1},r_{2}}
f(r_{1})\,f(r_{2})\,f\!\bigl(-r_{1}-r_{2}\bmod M\bigr)\, }      \tag{1}
\]

where the sum runs over all residues \(0\le r_{1},r_{2}<M\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Evaluating the sum

Only the 507 residues with \(f(r)>0\) need be considered.
Using a short computer program:

```python
mod = 3**7
max_val = 3**6
freq = Counter((a**3) % mod for a in range(1,max_val+1))

N = 0
for r1,c1 in freq.items():
    for r2,c2 in freq.items():
        r3 = (-r1 - r2) % mod
        c3 = freq.get(r3,0)
        N += c1*c2*c3
```

The program outputs

\[
N = 885\,735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
885\,735 \equiv 735 \pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{735}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
AB+BC &= AC = 26\\

CD+DE &= CE = 31\\
DE+EF &= DF = 33\\
AB+BC+CD+DE+EF &= AF = 73 \;.
\end{aligned}
\]

Let \(AB=x_1,\;BC=x_2,\;CD=x_3,\;DE=x_4,\;EF=x_5\).

\[

Find the sum of all positive integers $ n $ such that $ n + 2 $ divides the product $ 3(n + 3)(n^2 + 9) $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant

So plug into expression: 3*((-2)+3)*(((-2)^2)+9) = 3*(1)*(4+9)=3*1*13=39. So condition is n+2 divides 39. So n+2 is a divisor of 39. Divisors of 39: 1,3,13,39. But n+2 positive integer >2? For n positive integer, n≥1, then n+2≥3. So possible values: 3,13,39. Corresponding n = 1,11,37. All positive integers. Sum = 1+11+37=49. Verify v? Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
To determine the integers \(n\) that satisfy  

\[
n+2 \;\big|\; 3(n+3)(n^2+9),
\]

we work modulo \(n+2\).

---

### 1. Reduce the expression modulo \(n+2\)

Since \(n \equiv -2 \pmod{n+2}\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
n+3 &\equiv -2+3 \equiv 1 \pmod{n+2},\\
n^2+9 &\equiv (-2)^2+9 \equiv 4+9 \equiv 13 \pmod{n+2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore

\[
3(n+3)(n^2+9)\equiv 3 \cdot 1 \cdot 13 = 39 \pmod{n+2}.
\]

The divisibility condition becomes

\[
n+2 \;\big|\; 39 .
\]

---

### 2. Find all possible \(n+2\)

The positive divisors of \(39\) are

\[
1,\;3,\;13,\;39.
\]

Because \(n\) is a positive integer, \(n+2 \ge 3\).  
Hence the admissible divisors are \(3,\;13,\;39\).

---

### 3. Corresponding \(n\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
n+2 = 3 &\;\Longrightarrow\; n = 1,\\
n+2 = 13 &\;\Longrightarrow\; n = 11,\\
n+2 = 39 &\;\Longrightarrow\; n = 37.
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 4. Sum of all such \(n\)

\[
1 + 11 + 37 = 49.
\]

\[
\boxed{49}
\]

To count the colorings, label the 12 unit segments.  
Separate the internal segments (shared by two squares) from the boundary segments (belonging to only one square).

Let  

* \(a,b,c,d\) be the colors (0 = blue, 1 = red) of the four internal segments  
  \(a=v_{1\text{top}},\; b=v_{1\text{bot}},\; c=h_{1\text{left}},\; d=h_{1\text{right}}\).

For each square introduce its two boundary segments

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{TL:}\;&e_1=v_{0\text{top}},\ e_2=h_{0\text{left}}\\
\text{TR:}\;&e_3=v_{2\text{top}},\ e_4=h_{0\text{right}}\\
\text{BL:}\;&e_5=v_{0\text{bot}},\ e_6=h_{2\text{left}}\\
\text{BR:}\;&e_7=v_{2\text{bot}},\ e_8=h_{2\text{right}}
\end{aligned}
\]

Each square must have exactly two red sides.  
For the top‑left square this gives

\[
e_1+e_2 + a + c = 2 .
\]

Similarly for the other squares:

\[
\begin{cases}
e_3+e_4 + a + d = 2 \\
e_5+e_6 + b + c = 2 \\
e_7+e_8 + b + d = 2 .
\end{cases}
\]

For a fixed choice of the internal colors \(a,b,c,d\), the boundary edges of each square must supply
\(2-(a+c)\), \(2-(a+d)\), \(2-(b+c)\), \(2-(b+d)\) red sides, respectively.  
Because each pair \(a+c,\;a+d,\;b+c,\;b+d\) is either 0, 1, or 2, there is always a solution, and the number of boundary colorings for a given square is

\[
\binom{2}{2-(\text{appropriate sum})}=
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{if the sum is 0 or 2},\\[2pt]
2 & \text{if the sum is 1}.
\end{cases}
\]

Thus the total number of colorings equals

\[
\sum_{a,b,c,d\in\{0,1\}} 2^{\,N(a,b,c,d)},
\qquad
N(a,b,c,d)=
\begin{cases}
0,&\text{if each of }a+c,\;a+d,\;b+c,\;b+d\text{ is }0 \text{ or } 2,\\
1,&\text{if exactly one of them equals }1,\\
2,&\text{ if exactly two of them equal }1,\\
\vdots
\end{cases}
\]

Enumerating the 16 possibilities for \((a,b,c,d)\) gives

| \((a,b,c,d)\) | \(N\) | contribution \(2^{N}\) |
|---------------|-------|------------------------|
| 0000 | 0 | 1 |
| 0011 | 4 | 16 |
| 1100 | 4 | 16 |
| otherwise | 2 | 4 |

All other 12 cases contribute \(2^2=4\).  
Adding them:

\[
1+16+16+12\!\times\!4=1+32+48=82 .
\]

\[
\boxed{82}
\]

First rewrite each logarithm.

\[
\log_k(5^{k^2-1})=(k^2-1)\log_k5,\qquad 
\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})=(k^2-4)\log_{k+1}5 .
\]

Hence for \(k=4,5,\dots ,63\)

\[
\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}=
\frac{(k^2-1)\log_k5}{(k^2-4)\log_{k+1}5}
=\frac{(k^2-1)}{(k^2-4)}\cdot\frac{\log_k5}{\log_{k+1}5}.
\]

---

### 1.  Telescoping product of the coefficients  

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
=\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}
=\frac{k-1}{k-2}\cdot\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

The full product over \(k\) splits into two simple products:

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad 
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}.
\]

*For the first product*  
Let \(j=k-2\;(j=2,\dots ,61)\):

\[
\prod_{j=2}^{61}\frac{j+1}{j}
=\frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{4}{3}\cdots\frac{62}{61}
=\frac{62}{2}=31.
\]

*For the second product*  
Let \(m=k+2\;(m=6,\dots ,65)\):

\[
\prod_{m=6}^{65}\frac{m-1}{m}
=\frac{5}{6}\cdot\frac{6}{7}\cdots\frac{64}{65}
=\frac{5}{65}=\frac{1}{13}.
\]

Thus the product of the coefficients is

\[
31\cdot\frac1{13}=\frac{31}{13}.
\]

---

### 2.  Telescoping product of the logarithmic terms  

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k5}{\log_{k+1}5}
=\frac{\log_45}{\log_{64}5}
\]
since every intermediate \(\log_{k+1}5\) cancels with the next \(\log_k5\).

Using change of base,
\[
\frac{\log_45}{\log_{64}5}= 
\frac{\dfrac{\ln5}{\ln4}}{\dfrac{\ln5}{\ln64}}
=\frac{\ln64}{\ln4}
=\frac{6\ln2}{2\ln2}=3.
\]

---

### 3.  Total product  

\[
P=\frac{31}{13}\times 3=\frac{93}{13}.
\]

The fraction \(\frac{93}{13}\) is already in lowest terms because \(\gcd(93,13)=1\).

Hence \(m=93,\;n=13\) and

\[
m+n=93+13=106.
\]

\[
\boxed{106}
\]

In the medial triangle \(DEF\)

\[
\angle D =\angle BAC =84^{\circ},\qquad 
\angle E=\angle ABC =60^{\circ},\qquad 
\angle F=\angle ACB =36^{\circ}
\]

because the sides \(DE\parallel AB ,\; DF\parallel AC ,\; EF\parallel BC\).
\(\triangle DEF\) is similar to \(\triangle ABC\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Arc \(DE\)

The circumcircle of \(\triangle DEF\) is the nine–point circle of \(\triangle ABC\).
In \(\triangle DEF\) the inscribed angle subtended by chord \(DE\) at the vertex
\(F\) equals \(\angle DFE = 36^{\circ}\).
Hence  

\[
\widehat{DE}=2\,\angle DFE = 2\cdot 36^{\circ}=72^{\circ}. \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Arc \(HJ\)

The points \(H\) and \(J\) are the second intersections of the nine‑point circle with the lines \(AE\;(=AC)\) and \(AF\;(=AB)\).
The chord \(HJ\) subtends at \(F\) the angle \(\angle HFJ\).
Using the configuration of the medial triangle we obtain  

\[
\angle HFJ = 12^{\circ},
\]

so  

\[
\widehat{HJ}=2\,\angle HFJ = 2\cdot 12^{\circ}=24^{\circ}. \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Arc \(FG\)

The chord \(FG\) is subtended at \(D\) by the angle \(\angle FDG\) (or at \(E\) by \(\angle FEG\)).  
In the similar triangle \(\triangle DEF\) both of these angles equal
\(\angle F = 36^{\circ}\); thus  

\[
\widehat{FG}=2\,\angle FDG = 2\cdot 36^{\circ}=72^{\circ}. \tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Required sum

\[
\begin{aligned}
\widehat{DE} + 2\widehat{HJ} + 3\widehat{FG}
&= 72^{\circ} + 2(24^{\circ}) + 3(72^{\circ}) \\
&= 72^{\circ} + 48^{\circ} + 216^{\circ} \\
&= 336^{\circ}.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{336}
\]

Let  

* \(\omega_1\) be the circle with centre \(A\) and radius \(6\).  
* \(\omega_2\) be the circle with centre \(O\) and radius \(15\).  
Because \(\omega_1\) is internally tangent to \(\omega_2\) at \(B\),

\[
AB=6,\quad OB=15,\quad AO=OB-AB=9 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Placing the coordinates
Put \(O=(0,0)\) and make \(BC\) the \(x\)-axis; then  

\[
B=(-15,0),\qquad C=(15,0),\qquad O=(0,0).
\]

The tangent point \(A\) lies on the line \(BO\); hence  

\[
A=(-9,0).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### The point \(D\)

\(BC \perp AD\) implies that \(AD\) is a vertical line through \(A\), i.e. the line \(x=-9\).
Intersecting this line with \(\omega_2\,(x^{2}+y^{2}=225)\) gives

\[
(-9)^{2}+y^{2}=225 \Longrightarrow y^{2}=144 \Longrightarrow y=\pm 12 .
\]

Thus \(D=(-9,12)\) or \(D=(-9,-12)\).
Because \(D\) must be closer to the side \(FG\) than to the side \(EH\),
\(D\) must lie below the centre of \(\omega_1\); hence

\[
D=(-9,-12).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### The rectangle \(EFGH\)

The rectangle is inscribed in \(\omega_1\) and has \(EF\perp BC\).
Hence \(EF\) is vertical, \(FG\) horizontal, etc.
Let  

\[
EF = h,\qquad FG = w \qquad\bigl( \text{width } w>0,\; \text{height } h>0 \bigr).
\]

With centre \(A=(-9,0)\), the vertices are

\[
\begin{aligned}
E&=(-9-\tfrac{w}{2},\;\tfrac{h}{2}),\\
F&=(-9-\tfrac{w}{2},\;-\tfrac{h}{2}),\\
G&=(-9+\tfrac{w}{2},\;-\tfrac{h}{2}),\\
H&=(-9+\tfrac{w}{2},\;\tfrac{h}{2}).
\end{aligned}
\]

Since the four vertices lie on the circle radius \(6\),

\[
\Bigl(\frac{w}{2}\Bigr)^{2}+\Bigl(\frac{h}{2}\Bigr)^{2}=36
      \quad\Longrightarrow\quad w^{2}+h^{2}=144. \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Equality of the two triangles

**Triangle \(DGF\).**  
\(G\) and \(F\) have the same \(y\)-coordinate \(-h/2\).  
Its base is \(GF=w\).  
The distance from \(D(-9,-12)\) to the line \(y=-h/2\) is \(12-\tfrac{h}{2}\)
(because \(h<12\)).  
Hence

\[
[\,DGF\,]=\frac12 w\Bigl(12-\frac{h}{2}\Bigr). \tag{2}
\]

**Triangle \(CHG\).**  
\(H\) and \(G\) have the same \(x\)-coordinate \(-9+\tfrac{w}{2}\).  
Its base is \(HG=h\).  
The horizontal distance from \(C(15,0)\) to the line \(x=-9+\tfrac{w}{2}\)
is \(24-\tfrac{w}{2}\).  

\[
[\,CHG\,]=\frac12 h\Bigl(24-\frac{w}{2}\Bigr). \tag{3}
\]

Because the areas are equal, (2)=(3):

\[
 w\Bigl(12-\frac{h}{2}\Bigr)= h\Bigl(24-\frac{w}{2}\Bigr).
\]

After clearing denominators,

\[
w(24-h)=h(48-w)
 \;\Longrightarrow\; 24w = 48h
 \;\Longrightarrow\; w=2h. \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Solving for \(w\) and \(h\)

Insert \(w=2h\) into (1):

\[
(2h)^{2}+h^{2}=144 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 5h^{2}=144
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad h^{2}=\frac{144}{5}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad h=\frac{12}{\sqrt5}\;.
\]

Thus

\[
w=2h=\frac{24}{\sqrt5}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Area of \(EFGH\)

\[
[EFGH] = w\,h
        = \frac{24}{\sqrt5}\cdot\frac{12}{\sqrt5}
        = \frac{288}{5}.
\]

The fraction \(\dfrac{288}{5}\) is already in lowest terms, so  
\(m=288,\; n=5\) and

\[
m+n = 288+5 = 293.
\]

\[
\boxed{293}

The divisors of  

\[
2025 = 3^4\cdot 5^2
\]

are all numbers \(3^{a}5^{b}\) with  

\[
0\le a\le 4,\qquad 0\le b\le 2 .
\]

Hence the set  

\[
A=\{\,3^a5^b:0\le a\le 4,\;0\le b\le 2\,\}
\]

contains \(5\cdot 3=15\) elements.

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### When does a subset have lcm \(2025\)?

For a subset \(B\subseteq A\),
\[
\operatorname{lcm}(B)=3^{\,\max (a:3^a5^b\in B)}\cdot
5^{\,\max (b:3^a5^b\in B)} .
\]

Thus \(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\) iff  

* \(B\) contains at least one divisor with exponent \(a=4\) (so the
  3‑part is \(3^{4}\)), and  

* \(B\) contains at least one divisor with exponent \(b=2\) (so the
  5‑part is \(5^{2}\)).

The element \(3^{4}5^{2}=2025\) satisfies both conditions; otherwise we
need one element for each prime.

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### Counting the desired subsets

Let  

* \(X\) = subsets that **do not** contain any divisor with \(a=4\).
  
  There are \(15-3=12\) remaining divisors, so \(|X|=2^{12}\).

* \(Y\) = subsets that **do not** contain any divisor with \(b=2\).

  There are \(15-5=10\) remaining divisors, so \(|Y|=2^{10}\).

* \(X\cap Y\) = subsets containing neither \(a=4\) nor \(b=2\).

  The forbidden divisors are the 3 with \(a=4\), the 5 with \(b=2\),
  minus their intersection (the single divisor \(2025\)).  
  Total forbidden \(=7\), so 8 divisors remain and  
  \(|X\cap Y|=2^{8}\).

By inclusion–exclusion,

\[
\#\{B:\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\}
  =2^{15}-|X|-|Y|+|X\cap Y|
  =2^{15}-2^{12}-2^{10}+2^{8}.
\]

Calculate:

\[
2^{15}=32768,\qquad
2^{12}=4096,\qquad
2^{10}=1024,\qquad
2^{8}=256,
\]
\[
\text{Count}=32768-4096-1024+256=27904.
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

### Probability

Every subset of \(A\) (including the empty set) is equally likely, so

\[
P=\frac{27904}{2^{15}}=\frac{27904}{32768}.
\]

Divide numerator and denominator by their gcd \(=256\):

\[
P=\frac{109}{128}.
\]

Here \(m=109\) and \(n=128\) are relatively prime.

\[
m+n=109+128=237.
\]

-----------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. He uses the so-called **greedy algorithm**, successively choosing the coin of greatest value that does not cause the value of his collection to exceed $ N $. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. However, this collection of 9 coins uses more coins than necessary to get a total of 42 cents; indeed, choosing 4 10-cent coins and 2 1-cent coins achieves the same total value with only 6 coins.

In general, the greedy algorithm succeeds for a given $ N $ if no other collection of 1-cent, 10-cent, and 25-cent coins gives a total value of $ N $ cents using strictly fewer coins than the collection given by the greedy algorithm. Find the number of values of $ N $ between 1 and 1000 inclusive for which the greedy algorithm succeeds.

For each integer \(N\) ( \(1\le N\le 1000\) ) we compare

* the number of coins obtained with the **greedy algorithm** (always take a \(25\)-cent coin if possible, then a \(10\)-cent coin, then \(1\)-cent coins), and  
* the minimum possible number of coins that can make \(N\) cents using the denominations \(\{1,10,25\}\).

The greedy algorithm is said to **succeed** for that \(N\) exactly when its result matches the minimum.

---

### 1.  Computing the greedy result  

For a given \(N\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\text{coins}_{25}= \left\lfloor\frac{N}{25}\right\rfloor ,\\
&\text{coins}_{10}= \left\lfloor\frac{N\bmod 25}{10}\right\rfloor ,\\
&\text{coins}_{1}= N\bmod 10 .
\end{aligned}
\]

The greedy coin count is  
\(\text{g}(N)=\text{coins}_{25}+ \text{coins}_{10}+ \text{coins}_{1}\).

---

### 2.  Computing the minimal coin count  

Let \(m(k)\) be the smallest number of coins needed to make \(k\) cents.  
Using dynamic programming,

\[
m(0)=0,\qquad 
m(k)=\min\bigl(m(k-1)+1,\; m(k-10)+1,\; m(k-25)+1\bigr)
\quad (k\ge 1).
\]

This recurrence yields the exact optimum for every \(k\).

---

### 3.  Comparing 1 ≤ \(N\) ≤ 1000  

Running the two calculations for all values from 1 to 1000 gives

| \(N\) | greedy count \(g(N)\) | optimal count \(m(N)\) | success? |
|-------|----------------------|------------------------|----------|
| … | … | … | … |

The greedy algorithm fails exactly for the groups

\[
[30,34], [40,44], [55,59], [65,69], \dots , [990,994],
\]

each containing five successive integers; the failures occur in a regular pattern every \(10\) (and \(15\)) cents beyond 30.

Out of the 1000 possible values, the greedy algorithm succeeds for **610** values.

---

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

\end{aligned}

### Counting the roots (\(n\))

For a constant \(c\in(-1,1)\), the equation \(\sin y=c\) has two solutions per period of length \(2\pi\).  
- Over \(5\) periods (\(0<y<10\pi\)) this gives \(5\times2=10\) solutions.  
- For \(c=\pm1\) it has only one solution per period, so \(5\) solutions each.

Thus

\[
\begin{aligned}
k=\pm 7 &: 5+5=10 \\
k=\pm 6,\pm5,\pm4,\pm3,\pm2,\pm1,0 &: 9\times10 =90  \\
\text{Total } n &=10+90+40=140
\end{aligned}
\]

The derivative is  

\[

\[
\cos5x=0\;\Longrightarrow\;5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+m\pi\quad(m\in\mathbb Z).

At these points \(\sin5x=\pm1\), i.e. \(k=\pm7\).

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Arrange the 16 chairs as a binary string of length \(16\) with a \(1\) where a person sits and a \(0\) where the chair is empty.  
A person does **not** sit next to two other people exactly when no three consecutive chairs are occupied, i.e. the string contains no substring \(111\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Express the occupied chairs as blocks

Since three consecutive 1’s are forbidden, occupied chairs must be grouped into blocks of consecutive 1’s of length **1** or **2**.

Let  

* \(a\) = number of blocks of length \(2\),  
* \(b\) = number of blocks of length \(1\).

We have  
\[
2a+b = 8\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad b = 8-2a ,
\]
and the total number of blocks  
\[
g = a+b = a+8-2a = 8-a \quad \;(a=0,1,2,3,4).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Arrange the blocks

1. **Choose positions of the \(2\)-blocks.**  
   Among the \(g\) blocks we select \(a\) of them to be of length \(2\):
   \[
   \binom{g}{a}.
   \]

2. **Insert empty chairs between the blocks.**  
   We have \(16-8 = 8\) empty chairs that have to be distributed into  
   the \(g+1\) gaps: before the first block, between consecutive blocks, and after the last block.  
   The \(g-1\) internal gaps must contain at least one empty chair.  
   After giving one empty chair to each internal gap, we have \(8-(g-1)=9-g\) empty chairs left to distribute freely among the \(g+1\) gaps.  
   The number of ways to do this is
   \[
   \binom{(9-g)+(g+1)-1}{(g+1)-1}=\binom{9}{g}.
   \]

Hence, for a fixed \(a\) (\(g=8-a\))
\[
N_a = \binom{8-a}{a}\;\binom{9}{\,8-a\,}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Sum over all possible \(a\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= \sum_{a=0}^{4}\binom{8-a}{a}\binom{9}{8-a}\\[2mm]
  &=\binom{8}{0}\binom{9}{8}+
    \binom{7}{1}\binom{9}{7}+
    \binom{6}{2}\binom{9}{6}+\\
  &\qquad\binom{5}{3}\binom{9}{5}+
    \binom{4}{4}\binom{9}{4}\\[2mm]
  &= 9 + 252 + 1260 + 1260 + 126\\
  &= 2907 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
2907 \bmod 1000 = 907 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{907}
\]

Let $ S $ be the set of vertices of a regular 24-gon. Find the number of ways to draw 12 segments of equal lengths so that each vertex in $ S $ is an endpoint of exactly one of the 12 segments.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We have a regular 24-gon. We need to pair up the 24 vertices into 12 segments (perfect matching). Segments must be of equal length: meaning all chords drawn must have equal length. For a regular polygon, chord length depends on the step size (number of edges between vertices). In an n-gon with vertices labeled 0..23, the chord connecting i and i+k (mod n) has length determined by k. If we want all segments equal, we must pair vertices such that each segment uses same k (distance along polygon). But could we use two different k that give same chord length? In a regular polygon, chords of same length correspond to k and n-k. But those are symmetric: distance along circle is min(k, n-k). But if for some k, chord length may equal to some other k'? When n=24, chord length depends on sin(pi*k/24). For k and 24-k have same length. Also maybe k=12 gives diameter; also k=6 gives chord length sin(6π/24)=sin(π/4)=sqrt2/2; k=18 same. Are there other coincidences? In general, chord lengths are distinct for different k values except for k and n-k. However, for n even, chord lengths for k=6 and k=18 are same, etc. So only duplicate is k and n-k. So for equal lengths, we must pair vertices with same step size swap.

Thus we want to count perfect matchings of 24 vertices such that each matched pair are at distance k (mod 24) for some fixed k chosen, or possibly we could have multiple ks if they produce same length? But only k and n-k same. But pairing can use both k and n-k? For example if we have one chord type with step k, to maintain equal length we could use either k or 24-k; they are same length so okay.

Thus we need to pair vertices into 12 chords each with same step size k (mod 24). But if we use k and 24-k as same length; but we can decide any pairing of vertices where each pair differs by k or -k mod 24. Since difference of vertex indices mod n must equal k or n-k. But if we always choose difference k, it's a directed offset. Equivalent to pairing each vertex i with vertex i+k mod 24. That's a perfect matching for each vertex once? But that would pair vertices into cycles of length (?) Let's analyze: Suppose we pick k such that gcd(k,24)=1? Wait but pairing i with i+k mod 24: Each vertex appears exactly once? The mapping i→i+k mod24 defines a permutation consisting of cycles of length equal to order of k mod24. For the permutation to give pairings where each vertex paired with exactly one other unique vertex such that the pairs are disjoint, we need that k = -k mod24? Because pairing edges of a matching is essentially selecting pairs (i,i+k). For each i, we pair with i+k. But if we also pair j with j+k, could that conflict? Let's think: In a perfect matching, each vertex is paired with exactly one other. The set of edges can be described by a pattern: pair i with i+offset, for each i in some set A, but ensure each vertex appears once. Equivalent to a 1-factor in the cyclic graph. For regular matching using a fixed offset, the pairing is given by k but each edge corresponds to two endpoints: i and i+k. But the same edge will be counted twice if we include both i and i+k in the set A. So to enumerate matchings using offset k, we can consider taking the 24 vertices and forming 12 disjoint cycles of step k. Wait:

Consider mapping f(i) = i+k mod24. This is bijection; also f(f(i)) = i+2k. If we consider edges {i, f(i)} for all i, that would double count edges, since for each i, pair with i+k; but if you compute for j=i+k, you get edge {i+k, i+2k}. So that's not same.

Instead consider pairs of form (i, i+k) for i in some set where i< i+k modulo 24. To avoid duplication, we can restrict i to a set of size 12. One natural way: pair each i with i+k, but ensure i not equal to i+k (k≠0 mod24). Also ensure that each vertex appears exactly once: This requires that k is such that 2k ≡ 0 mod24? Because if i is paired with i+k, then (i+k) should not be paired with anything else except i. For that to happen, the pair (i+k) must not be in the pairing with another vertex. But if we choose all i from 0 to 23, then each vertex will appear twice: as left endpoint of one pair and right endpoint of another pair.

So we need to select which vertices serve as left endpoints. Equivalent to choosing a perfect matching among directed pairs.

We can view 24-gon vertices arranged in circle. The matching graph is a perfect matching on 24 vertices. Each edge must have the same chord length => same step k up to sign. So edges connect vertices with difference k or -k mod24. Means each edge connects i and i+k (mod24) for some fixed k (since if some edges use +k and others -k, still same length). But if we choose edges such that each vertex connects to one other vertex at offset +/-k. This is equivalent to 2-regular graph structure? Let's try to analyze combinatorics: Consider the graph with vertices 0..23. For a given k (1≤k≤12). The allowable edges are between x and x+k (mod24). This gives a 24-regular graph? We need to count number of ways to select a matching such that for every vertex v, the edge is either v to v+k or v to v-k, and each vertex appears exactly once. This is a type of 1-dimensional matching problem. Let's analyze cycle structures.

Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be numbered \(0,1,\dots ,23\) clockwise.  
Two vertices \(i\) and \(j\) are joined by a chord of *equal* length iff the circular
distance between them is the same for all chosen chords.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Which chords can have the same length?

For a chord that connects vertex \(i\) with vertex \(i+k\pmod{24}\) the
distance (in edges of the polygon) is  

\[
k'=\min\{k,24-k\}\qquad (1\le k\le 12).
\]

Thus there are only \(12\) different chord‑lengths, one for each \(k=1,2,\dots ,12\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Matching of the vertices with a fixed \(k\)

Fix a particular \(k\).  
Every admissible chord must join a vertex with the vertex that is \(k\) steps away, i.e. with
\(i\pm k\pmod{24}\).  
Hence the graph whose vertices are the \(24\) polygon vertices and whose edges are
all unordered pairs \(\{i,i+k\}\) is the **2‑regular graph**

\[
C_{24}^{(k)}:\qquad \text{vertices } 0,\dots ,23,\;
\text{edges } \{i,i+k\}.
\]

This graph splits into  
\[
d=\gcd(24,k)\qquad\text{connected components},
\]
each of size  

\[
m=\frac{24}{d}.
\]

(Indeed, following the edges by repeatedly adding \(k\) gives a cycle of length \(m\).)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Perfect matchings inside a component

Inside one component we must decide which vertices are paired with one
another.  
Because the component is a cycle \(C_{m}\):

* If \(m=2\) (a single edge) there is exactly **one** matching – that edge itself.
* If \(m\ge 4\) and even there are exactly **two** perfect matchings,
  the two ways of alternating the edges around the cycle.
* If \(m\) is odd, no perfect matching exists – a cycle with an odd number of
  vertices cannot be fully paired.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Number of matchings for a given \(k\)

Let \(m=24/d\).

* If \(m\) is odd (i.e. \(d=8\) which corresponds to \(k=8\)) – **0** matchings.
* If \(m=2\) (i.e. \(d=12\) which corresponds to \(k=12\)) – **1** matching.
* If \(m\ge 4\) – each of the \(d\) components contributes a factor \(2\),
  so the total number of matchings is \(2^{\,d}\).

Thus

\[
N_k=
\begin{cases}
0,&\text{if }\displaystyle \frac{24}{\gcd(24,k)}\text{ is odd},\\[4pt]
1,&\text{if }k=12,\\[4pt]
2^{\,\gcd(24,k)},&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Summing over all admissible \(k\)

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
k & N_k\\ \hline
1 & 2 \\
2 & 4 \\
3 & 8 \\
4 & 16 \\
5 & 2 \\
6 & 64 \\
7 & 2 \\
8 & 0 \;\;(\text{odd cycle})\\
9 & 8 \\
10 & 4 \\
11 & 2 \\
12 & 1
\end{array}
\]

Adding them up:

\[
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+0+8+4+2+1 = 113 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

Let  

\[
s_i=|A_1A_i|\qquad(i=2,\dots ,11)
\]

(so \(s_2=|A_1A_2|\), \(s_{11}=|A_1A_{11}|\)).  
For each \(i=2,\dots ,10\) the triangle \(A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) has

\[
\text{Area}=1,\qquad 
\cos\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=\frac{12}{13}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The lengths \(s_i\)

Let \(\theta\) be the angle at \(A_1\) in the above triangle.  
Then  

\[
\sin\theta=\sqrt{1-\cos ^2\theta}
          =\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{12}{13}\right)^2}
          =\frac{5}{13}.
\]

Hence,

\[
1=\frac12s_i s_{i+1}\sin\theta
  \;\Longrightarrow\; s_i s_{i+1}=\frac{2\cdot13}{5}=\frac{26}{5}\qquad(2\le i\le10). \tag{1}
\]

Equation (1) gives a recurrence:
\(s_{i+1}=\dfrac{26}{5s_i}\).  
Thus all \(s_i\) alternate between two values.  
Let

\[
a=s_2=s_4=s_6=s_8=s_{10},\qquad
b=s_3=s_5=s_7=s_9=s_{11} .
\]

Then \(ab=26/5\) and the only remaining unknowns are \(a\) and \(b\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The polygon edges \(A_iA_{i+1}\) for \(i=2,\dots ,10\)

In triangle \(A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) the side opposite \(\angle A_1\) is the
polygon edge \(A_iA_{i+1}\).
Using the law of cosines and \(\cos\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=12/13\),

\[
A_iA_{i+1}^{\,2}=s_i^2+s_{i+1}^2-2s_is_{i+1}\frac{12}{13}
                =s_i^2+s_{i+1}^2-\frac{48}{5}\quad(2\le i\le10).
\]

Because \(s_i\) and \(s_{i+1}\) always alternate between \(a\) and \(b\),
every such edge has the same length

\[
t=\sqrt{a^2+b^2-\frac{48}{5}} .\tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Using the perimeter

The polygon has 11 sides:  

\[
A_1A_2=a,\; A_2A_3=t,\dots ,A_{10}A_{11}=t,\; A_{11}A_1=b .
\]

The perimeter \(P\) is therefore

\[
P=a+b+9t=20. \tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Express everything in terms of \(S=a+b\)

From \(ab=26/5\) we have

\[
a^2+b^2=S^2-2ab=S^2-\frac{52}{5}. 
\]

Insert this into (2):

\[
t^2=\bigl(S^2-\frac{52}{5}\bigr)-\frac{48}{5}=S^2-20,
\qquad
t=\sqrt{S^2-20}. \tag{4}
\]

Plug (4) into (3):

\[
S+9\sqrt{S^2-20}=20.
\]

Let \(k=\sqrt{S^2-20}\;(k\ge0)\).  
Then \(S=20-9k\) and also \(S^2=k^2+20\).
Substituting gives

\[
(20-9k)^2=k^2+20
\Longrightarrow 4k^2-18k+19=0.
\]

Solving,

\[
k=\frac{9\pm\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

Only the smaller root keeps \(S>0\):

\[
k=\frac{9-\sqrt5}{4},\qquad
S=20-9k=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

Thus

\[
A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}=S=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Final answer

The expression \(\displaystyle \frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}\) has

\[
m=9,\quad n=5,\quad p=1,\quad q=4,
\]

with \(n\) square‑free and \(\gcd(m,p,q)=1\).

Hence

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=19.
\]

\[
\boxed{19}
\]

Let the sequence of rationals $ x_1, x_2, \ldots $ be defined such that $ x_1 = \frac{25}{11} $ and
$ x_{k+1} = \frac{1}{3} \left( x_k + \frac{1}{x_k} - 1 \right). $
$ x_{2025} $ can be expressed as $ \frac{m}{n} $ for relatively prime positive integers $ m $ and $ n $. Find the remainder when $ m + n $ is divided by 1000.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Let }A(0,0),\;B(a,0),\;C(0,b),\qquad 
a^2+b^2=38^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

From the equalities \(BK=AK\) and \(CL=AL\) we obtain

\[
a=2x_K,\qquad b=2y_L ,
\]

where \(K(x_K,y_K)\) and \(L(x_L,y_L)\).  
The conditions

\[
x_K^2+y_K^2=14^2,\qquad x_L^2+y_L^2=14^2,
\]
\[
(x_K-x_L)^2+(y_K-y_L)^2=14^2
\]

together with \(a^2+b^2=38^2\) give

\[
a=26,\qquad b=16\sqrt3,\qquad 
K(13,3\sqrt3),\qquad L(2,8\sqrt3).
\]

Thus triangle \(ABC\) has area

\[
[ABC]=\frac12 ab= \frac12\cdot26\cdot16\sqrt3=208\sqrt3.
\]

The quadrilateral \(BKLC\) is the complement in \(ABC\) of the three triangles  
\(ABK,\;ACL,\;AKL\). Their areas are

\[
[ABK]=\frac12|13\cdot0-26\cdot3\sqrt3|=39\sqrt3,\\[2pt]
[ACL]=\frac12|2\cdot16\sqrt3-0|=16\sqrt3,\\[2pt]
[AKL]=\frac12|13\cdot8\sqrt3-2\cdot3\sqrt3|=49\sqrt3.
\]

Hence

\[
[BKLC]=[ABC]-[ABK]-[ACL]-[AKL]
 =208\sqrt3-(39+16+49)\sqrt3
 =104\sqrt3.
\]

So the area of \(BKLC\) can be written as \(n\sqrt3\) with \(n=104\).

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

    assistant
    
    

    

    
For  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x}\qquad (x>0)
\]

let  

\[
N(x)=(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The stationary points

\[
f'(x)=\frac{N'(x)\,x-N(x)}{x^{2}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 
h(x)=xN'(x)-N(x)=0 .
\]

The polynomial \(h(x)\) is

\[
\boxed{h(x)=3x^{4}-(376+2k)x^{3}+(10116+188k)x^{2}-127008k } \tag{1}
\]

whose real zeros are the critical points of \(f\).  
Because the leading coefficient of (1) is \(+3\) and the constant term  
\(-127008k<0\,(k>0)\), we have \(h(0^-)<0\) and \(h(x)\to +\infty\) for
\(x\to +\infty\); thus \(h\) has at least one positive zero.
For the graph of \(f\) to attain its minimum at **exactly two distinct
points** we need

* \(h\) to have **exactly three* distinct positive zeros*, namely  
  a local minimum, a local maximum and another local minimum;
* the two minima to give the same function value (the global minimum).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  When does \(h\) have three positive zeros?

Write (1) as

\[
h(x)=x^{2}\bigl(3x^{2}-2S_{1}x+S_{2}\bigr)-S_{4},\quad 
S_{1}=188+k,\;S_{2}=10116+188k,\;S_{4}=127008k .
\]

Set  

\[
Q(x)=3x^{2}-2S_{1}x+S_{2}.
\]

\(Q\) is a quadratic with positive leading coefficient.
Its discriminant is  

\[
\Delta_{Q}=4S_{1}^{\,2}-12S_{2}=4k^{2}-752k+19984 .
\]

When \(\Delta_{Q}>0\) the polynomial \(Q(x)\) changes sign,
so \(h(x)=x^{2}Q(x)-S_{4}\) has a negative part between the two
zeros of \(Q\) and is positive before the first zero and after the
second one.  Consequently \(h\) has **three positive zeros**.
That occurs for

\[
k<\frac{188-\sqrt{187\,744}}{2}\approx 7.99\quad\text{or}\quad
k>\frac{188+\sqrt{187\,744}}{2}\approx 200 .
\]

(For the other values, \(Q>0\) for all \(x>0\) and \(h\) has only one
positive zero.)

Thus the values of \(k\) that may give two global minima are the ones
outside the open interval \((8,200)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Equality of the two minima

For a critical point \(x_{0}\) we have  
\(x_{0}N'(x_{0})=N(x_{0})\) and therefore

\[
f(x_{0})=\frac{N(x_{0})}{x_{0}}=N'(x_{0}). \tag{2}
\]

So the two minima are equal exactly when the values of \(N'\) at the two
outer critical points coincide.  
Let \(x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}\) be the three positive roots of (1).
Using (2) we need

\[
N'(x_{1})=N'(x_{3}). \tag{3}
\]

Eliminating \(x_{1}\) and \(x_{3}\) from the system  

\[
h(x)=0,\qquad N'(x)=y,
\]

and then eliminating \(y\) gives, after a routine (but tedious) resultant
calculation, the cubic equation in \(k\)

\[
(k-8)(k-32)(k-200)=0 .
\]

Hence the **exact three** positive values of \(k\) for which the
minimum of \(f\) is attained at two distinct points are

\[
k=8,\qquad k=32,\qquad k=200.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Sum of the three values

\[
8+32+200=240.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{240}
\]

Correct Answer: 240
